The [Boeing X-32][ref_x32] is the only aircraft in this series that was built to
**lose or win a competition** rather than to answer a research question, and it lost. Every other X-plane so
far existed to find something out. The [X-31][related_post_a328_rockwell_mbb_x31] flew adversarial
engagements to learn whether post-stall manoeuvring wins. The [X-30][related_post_a327_rockwell_x30] was
cancelled because its central quantity could not be established. **The X-32 was a competitor**, flown
against the [Lockheed Martin X-35][ref_x35] in a fly-off for the largest military aircraft procurement in
history, and on 26 October 2001 the Department of Defense chose the other one. This is the thirty-third
article in the [X-Planes series][related_post_a297_xplanes_framing], following the
[X-1][related_post_a298_bell_x1], the [X-2][related_post_a299_bell_x2], the
[X-3][related_post_a300_douglas_x3], the [X-4][related_post_a301_northrop_x4], the
[X-5][related_post_a302_bell_x5], the [X-6][related_post_a303_convair_x6], the
[X-7][related_post_a304_lockheed_x7], the [X-8][related_post_a305_aerojet_x8], the
[X-9][related_post_a306_bell_x9], the [X-10][related_post_a307_north_american_x10], the
[X-11][related_post_a308_convair_x11], the [X-12][related_post_a309_convair_x12], the
[X-13][related_post_a310_ryan_x13], the [X-14][related_post_a311_bell_x14], the
[X-15][related_post_a312_north_american_x15], the [X-16][related_post_a313_bell_x16], the
[X-17][related_post_a314_lockheed_x17], the [X-18][related_post_a315_hiller_x18], the
[X-19][related_post_a316_curtiss_wright_x19], the [X-20][related_post_a317_boeing_x20], the
[X-21][related_post_a318_northrop_x21], the [X-22][related_post_a319_bell_x22], the
[X-23][related_post_a320_martin_marietta_x23], the [X-24][related_post_a321_martin_marietta_x24], the
[X-25][related_post_a322_bensen_x25], the [X-26][related_post_a323_schweizer_x26], the
[X-27][related_post_a324_lockheed_x27], the [X-28][related_post_a325_osprey_x28], the
[X-29][related_post_a326_grumman_x29], the [X-30][related_post_a327_rockwell_x30], and the
[X-31][related_post_a328_rockwell_mbb_x31].

**The easy misreading is that the X-32 lost because direct lift does not work.** It works. It had been
working since the Hawker Siddeley [Harrier][ref_harrier] entered service in 1969, and the X-32B hovered and
landed vertically. Nothing about the mechanism was in doubt.

**What was being decided was something else, and it has two halves.** The first is a question in propulsion
physics with a clean answer that this article derives from published numbers, resting on a research base
that the National Aeronautics and Space Administration had been building since the 1970s. The second is a
question about evidence, namely whether two demonstrators,
**neither of which was the aeroplane being bought**, could settle a choice about an aircraft that did not
yet exist.

* Contents
{:toc}

## The Research Question

**The keystone is what a fly-off decides and on what evidence.**

A research aircraft is dimensioned against an unknown. A competitor is dimensioned against a **rival**, and
the two are not the same discipline. The X-32 had to be good enough at everything and better at something,
against an opponent whose design it could not see, under a set of criteria written by a customer buying
three different aircraft for three different services.

**The binding question is therefore evidentiary rather than physical.** Both companies could build an
aeroplane that hovered. What neither could do was build the aeroplane it was proposing, because the
demonstrators were concept demonstrators, funded to reduce risk rather than to be prototypes.
**A demonstrator that differs from the proposal in the feature under test is evidence about something other than the proposal**,
and this article's central historical finding is that the X-32's wing was exactly such a feature.

### The Physical Half, Which Has a Clean Answer

**Two ways exist to hold a fighter up on its engine.** The direct-lift approach sends the engine's own
exhaust downward through vectoring nozzles, which is the [Harrier's][ref_harrier] method and was Boeing's.
The lift-fan approach takes shaft power off the engine, drives a large fan mounted behind the cockpit, and
adds a second cold stream, which was Lockheed Martin's.

**Those are not two arrangements of the same thing. They are two points on a curve, and the curve has a known shape.**
The whole of the propulsion argument in this article follows from one relation derived in the sizing section
below, and the relation is not in dispute. It has been in the textbooks since momentum theory was written
down.

### The Evidentiary Half, Which Does Not

**The second half is harder and it is where this article spends its effort.** A fly-off produces one
comparison. It is not a sample, it is not repeatable, and the thing compared is not the thing being bought.
Asking what such an exercise establishes is a question about
[whether the quantity measured is the quantity that matters][ref_construct_validity], rather than about
sampling error.

**The previous article in this series asked a related question and got a different kind of answer.** The
X-31's combat evaluation flew hundreds of engagements and the difficulty was that a finite sample carries a
wide interval. Here the sample size is one, and the difficulty is that the one trial was not a trial of the
proposed aircraft.

The acquisition literature on what competitive prototyping can establish is gathered in the cluster on defence acquisition, competition, prototyping and cost under The Contemporary Literature.

## Programme Origin

### Three Aircraft, One Airframe

**The [Joint Strike Fighter programme][ref_jsf] set out to build one aircraft for three services with three incompatible requirements.**
The Air Force wanted a conventional runway fighter to replace the F-16. The Navy wanted a carrier aircraft,
which means a stronger structure, a bigger wing and an approach speed low enough to catch a wire. The Marine
Corps and the Royal Navy wanted a [short takeoff and vertical landing][ref_stovl] aircraft to replace the
Harrier, which means carrying a lift system that the other two variants would pay for in weight and volume
and never use.

**The STOVL requirement is the one that drives everything**, because it is the only one that cannot be met
by scaling. A carrier aircraft is a heavier version of a land aircraft. A vertical-landing aircraft is a
different aeroplane wearing the same skin.

The literature of commonality between variants is gathered in the cluster on commonality, product families and requirements under The Contemporary Literature.

### The Programme That Preceded It

The competition descends from two earlier efforts. **The Common Affordable Lightweight Fighter** work at the
[Defense Advanced Research Projects Agency][ref_darpa] explored whether a single design could serve several
services, and **the Joint Advanced Strike Technology** programme consolidated a set of service-specific
studies into one. The concept demonstration contracts were awarded to [Boeing][ref_boeing] and
[Lockheed Martin][ref_lockheed] in November 1996, and McDonnell Douglas, whose entry used a gas-coupled lift
fan, was eliminated at that point.

**That elimination is worth noting because it means the fly-off was already the second round.** The field
had been narrowed from three to two before either surviving aircraft existed.

The programme's own literature, from the Joint Advanced Strike Technology studies onward, is gathered in the cluster on the Joint Strike Fighter programme and its demonstrators under The Contemporary Literature.

### What Each Company Brought

Boeing's design used a single large delta wing with [55 degrees of leading-edge sweep][ref_x32], a chin inlet under the
nose, and a direct-lift STOVL system in which a valve diverted the core exhaust to a pair of vectoring
nozzles near the centre of gravity.

Lockheed Martin's design used a conventional wing and tail, a [shaft-driven lift fan][ref_liftsystem] behind
the cockpit, a three-bearing swivel module at the tail, and roll posts in the wings.

**The chin inlet is a consequence rather than a choice.** A direct-lift aircraft must put its lift nozzles
at the centre of gravity, which puts the engine amidships, which puts the inlet forward and low.
**The whole configuration follows from the propulsion decision**, which is the first sign that the two
entries were not two styles of the same aeroplane.

The lineages each company drew on are gathered in the clusters on lift fans, lift engines, ejectors and remote lift and on the Harrier, the Pegasus engine and the vectored-thrust lineage under The Contemporary Literature.

## Sizing From First Principles

**This section derives the relation the competition turned on, checks three published numbers against each other, and computes the quantity the decision was actually about.**

### The Relation

Static thrust is mass flow times jet velocity,

$$T = \dot{m} V_j$$

and the power added to the stream is the rate of increase of its kinetic energy,

$$P = \tfrac{1}{2} \dot{m} V_j^{2}$$

Eliminating the velocity between them gives the relation that governs the choice,

$$T = \sqrt{2 P \dot{m}}$$

**For a fixed power, thrust rises as the square root of the mass flow.** Equivalently, writing it the other
way,

$$T = \frac{2P}{V_j}$$

**halving the jet velocity doubles the thrust for the same power.**

| Mass flow ratio | Thrust ratio at fixed power | Power ratio at fixed thrust |
|---|---|---|
| 1.00 | 1.000 | 1.000 |
| 2.00 | 1.414 | 0.500 |
| 2.24 | 1.497 | 0.446 |
| 4.00 | 2.000 | 0.250 |

**That table is the entire case for the lift fan and it fits on four lines.** A lift fan spends shaft power
accelerating a large cold mass flow slowly. A direct-lift system spends the same power accelerating a
smaller hot flow quickly. The first produces more thrust from the same core, and everything else that
separated the two aircraft follows from it.

The modern literature of momentum theory is gathered in the cluster on momentum theory, rotors, propellers and hover performance under The Contemporary Literature.

### The Disc-Loading Form of the Same Statement

The relation is more useful expressed through the area the flow passes through, and the
[Azuma and Kawachi 1975][research_azuma_kawachi_1975] that governs it has been the standard treatment of a
static disc since the 1970s, with the [actuator-disc optimum in hover][research_modarres_peters_2015] worked
out more recently. It is the [disc loading][ref_disc_loading], which
[momentum theory][ref_momentum_theory] relates to the induced velocity. The relations used here are the
standard ones set out in [Leishman on helicopter aerodynamics][book_leishman] and in
[Hill and Peterson on propulsion][book_hill_peterson]. For a disc of area $A$ producing thrust $T$ in still
air, momentum theory gives the velocity induced at the disc,

$$V_i = \sqrt{\frac{T}{2 \rho A}}$$

and the flow reaches twice that velocity far downstream. The ideal power is the thrust times the induced
velocity,

$$P_{\text{ideal}} = T V_i = T \sqrt{\frac{T}{2 \rho A}}$$

**So the power required for a given lift rises as the square root of the disc loading.** A system that
pushes its air through a small hole pays for it in power, in exhaust velocity, in noise, in ground erosion
and in temperature.

### Three Published Numbers That Were Never Published Together

**The lift fan's diameter, its cold thrust and its shaft power appear in different places, and taken together they imply a figure of merit that has to be a number a real fan can achieve.**

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| Lift fan diameter | 50 inches |
| Disc area | 13.64 sq ft |
| Cold thrust | 20,000 lbf |
| Shaft power delivered | 29,000 hp |

The disc loading is 1,467 pounds per square foot, the induced velocity is

$$V_i = \sqrt{\frac{20{,}000}{2 (0.0023769)(13.64)}} = 555.5 \ \text{ft/s}$$

with a far-field velocity of 1,110.9 feet per second, and the ideal power is

$$P_{\text{ideal}} = (20{,}000)(555.5) = 1.111 \times 10^{7} \ \text{ft lbf/s} = 20{,}199 \ \text{hp}$$

That is 20,199 horsepower of ideal power. Dividing by the delivered shaft power gives

$$\text{FM} = \frac{20{,}199}{29{,}000} = 0.697$$

The same two relations fix the mass flow the fan moves, since the thrust is the mass flow times the
far-field velocity,

$$\dot{m} = \frac{T}{2 V_i} = \frac{20{,}000}{2(555.5)} = 18.00 \ \text{slug/s} = 579 \ \text{lb/s}$$

and the power loading follows as 0.690 pounds of thrust per horsepower delivered.

**A ducted lift fan cannot exceed a figure of merit of about 0.8**, and values near 0.7 are what good ones
achieve. The measure is the standard one used in [rotor design optimisation][research_bohorquez_pines_2010].
**The three numbers are mutually consistent**, which is worth establishing before building an argument on
them, and which nobody appears to have checked in print.

### The Nozzle the Direct-Lift System Would Have Needed

**Rather than assume what the X-32B's nozzles were, invert the question.** What nozzle area would the
direct-lift system have needed for its jets to match the lift fan's disc loading?

$$A = \frac{T}{T/A} = \frac{28{,}000}{1{,}467} = 19.09 \ \text{sq ft}$$

That is a single circular nozzle **4.93 feet across**, or two nozzles each **3.49 feet across**, on a
fuselage 45.0 feet long and 17.3 feet tall.

**That is not an engineering trade. It is a geometric impossibility.** There is nowhere on a single-engine
fighter to put two three-and-a-half-foot nozzles at the centre of gravity, and the exhaust passing through
them would be hot rather than cold in any case.

Taking plausible nozzle sizes instead shows how far from the fan the direct-lift system had to sit.

| Two nozzles | Total area | Disc loading | Induced velocity | Loading against the fan |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 2.0 ft across | 6.28 sq ft | 4,456 lb/sq ft | 968.2 ft/s | 3.04 |
| 2.5 ft across | 9.82 sq ft | 2,852 lb/sq ft | 774.6 ft/s | 1.94 |
| 3.0 ft across | 14.14 sq ft | 1,981 lb/sq ft | 645.5 ft/s | 1.35 |

### The Disc Loading Is Also the Pressure on the Ground

**The table above says more than it appears to, because of an identity the article had already assembled without stating.**
Momentum theory gives the disc loading as

$$\frac{T}{A} = 2 \rho V_i^{2}$$

and the flow reaches twice the induced velocity far downstream, so the dynamic pressure in the fully
developed jet is

$$q_{\infty} = \tfrac{1}{2} \rho (2 V_i)^{2} = 2 \rho V_i^{2} = \frac{T}{A}$$

**The disc loading and the dynamic pressure in the jet are the same number**, which is the quantity the
[jet impingement ground-effect][research_zhang_ing_1993] literature measures directly and which its
[computational treatment][research_zhang_ing_1994] reproduces. A table of disc loadings is therefore also a
table of the pressure each architecture imposes on whatever it is hovering over.

| System | Disc loading | Dynamic pressure at the ground | Against the fan |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lift fan | 1,467 lb/sq ft | 1,467 lb/sq ft | 1.00 |
| Two nozzles 3.0 ft across | 1,981 lb/sq ft | 1,981 lb/sq ft | 1.35 |
| Two nozzles 2.5 ft across | 2,852 lb/sq ft | 2,852 lb/sq ft | 1.94 |
| Two nozzles 2.0 ft across | 4,456 lb/sq ft | 4,456 lb/sq ft | 3.04 |

**Deck coatings, runway surfaces and the people standing near the aeroplane all feel that difference**, and
it costs nothing extra to derive because it is the same quantity the propulsion argument already needed.

**The lift fan is not a better version of a vectoring nozzle. It is a different point on the curve, and the curve is steep.**

### What the Two Architectures Actually Produced

The published totals make the consequence plain. The engine's military rating is from
[the X-32's published specification][ref_x32], and the lift system's components and total are from
[the LiftSystem's published component thrusts][ref_liftsystem].

| System | Lift | Source of lift |
|---|---|---|
| [Rolls-Royce Pegasus][ref_pegasus], for reference | 23,800 lbf | Vectored fan and core |
| Direct lift, engine military rating | 28,000 lbf | Entirely hot core stream |
| Lift fan plus swivel module plus roll posts | 41,900 lbf | 47.7 percent cold |

**The lift system exceeds the Pegasus by a factor of 1.76 and the direct-lift core by 1.18.** The lift fan
alone contributes 20,000 pounds, the swivel module 18,000 and the roll posts 3,900, and the published total
is exactly the sum of its published parts.

The partition is exact,

$$L = T_{\text{fan}} + T_{\text{3BSM}} + T_{\text{posts}} = 20{,}000 + 18{,}000 + 3{,}900 = 41{,}900 \ \text{lbf}$$

with shares of 47.7, 43.0 and 9.3 percent,
**and the published total is precisely the sum of its published parts**, which is a small check worth making
before relying on any of the four numbers.

**Nearly half of the winning aircraft's vertical lift came from air that never went through the engine.**
That is the square-root law made physical.

The wider literature of engine cycles and thrust is gathered in the cluster on engine cycles, variable cycle engines and engine performance under The Contemporary Literature.

### The Number the Competition Was Actually About

**A vertical landing is not a demonstration. It is an operational requirement with a weight attached**,
because an aircraft returning from a mission with unexpended weapons has to be able to land with them
aboard. The quantity that matters is what can be brought back.

The landing weight is bounded by the lift divided by a control margin, and what is available for fuel and
stores is whatever is left after the empty weight is paid,

$$W_{\text{land}} = \frac{L}{k} \qquad \text{and} \qquad W_{\text{allowance}} = \frac{L}{k} - W_{\text{empty}}$$

At an [empty weight of 24,030 pounds][ref_x32] and an assumed five percent control margin, so that
$k = 1.05$,

| Architecture | Lift | Maximum landing weight | Allowance over empty |
|---|---|---|---|
| Direct lift, core only | 28,000 lbf | 26,667 lb | **2,637 lb** |
| Lift fan plus core plus posts | 41,900 lbf | 39,905 lb | **15,875 lb** |

**The difference is 13,238 pounds and the ratio is 6.02.** The empty weight cancels from the difference,
which depends only on the two lifts $L_{\text{fan}}$ and $L_{\text{direct}}$ and the margin,

$$\Delta W_{\text{allowance}} = \frac{L_{\text{fan}} - L_{\text{direct}}}{k} = \frac{41{,}900 - 28{,}000}{1.05} = 13{,}238 \ \text{lb} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \frac{15{,}875}{2{,}637} = 6.02$$

**That is the competition in one line.** Both aircraft can hover. One of them can come home with two and a
half thousand pounds of fuel and weapons and the other with nearly sixteen thousand.

### Why the Allowance Is Badly Conditioned

**The allowance is a small difference between two large numbers, and that makes it fragile in a way the raw thrust figures conceal.**
Differentiating,

$$\frac{\partial W_{\text{allowance}} / W_{\text{allowance}}}{\partial L / L} = \frac{L/k}{W_{\text{allowance}}}$$

which is the landing weight divided by the allowance.

| Architecture | Amplification | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| Direct lift | 10.11 | One percent of thrust is ten percent of the allowance |
| Lift fan | 2.51 | One percent of thrust is two and a half percent |

**The same physical loss costs the direct-lift aircraft four times as much of what it can bring home.**

Because the X-32B's STOVL lift is not published, the conclusion is tested against the assumption rather than
resting on it. The engine's military rating is an **upper** bound, since a direct-lift arrangement loses
thrust in the ducting and the vanes.

| Assumed lift | Allowance | After a 50 degree inlet rise | Amplification |
|---|---|---|---|
| 24,000 lbf | −1,173 lb | −2,201 lb | not meaningful |
| 26,000 lbf | 732 lb | −382 lb | 33.83 |
| 28,000 lbf | 2,637 lb | 1,437 lb | 10.11 |
| 30,000 lbf | 4,541 lb | 3,256 lb | 6.29 |
| 32,000 lbf | 6,446 lb | 5,076 lb | 4.73 |

**The allowance is a straight line in the lift**, of slope one over the margin,

$$W_{\text{allowance}} = \frac{1}{k} L - W_{\text{empty}} = 0.9524 \, L - 24{,}030$$

crossing zero at

$$L_{\text{break even}} = k \, W_{\text{empty}} = 1.05 (24{,}030) = 25{,}232 \ \text{lbf}$$

**Below that lift the allowance is negative**, meaning the aircraft could not land vertically at its own
empty weight, let alone with anything in it.

**The amplification blows up as the allowance approaches zero, and that is not a numerical artefact but the shape of the problem.**
A direct-lift fighter of this size operates near a singularity in its own bring-back capability.
**The honest statement is not that the amplification is 10.11 but that the quantity is badly conditioned across the whole plausible range**,
and that no plausible assumption rescues it.

## The Vehicle

**The published table is thinner than for any flown aircraft in this series, and the sources disagree with one another on a basic dimension.**
Every figure in the table except the second span is from [the X-32's published specification][ref_x32], and
the second span is the 9.15 metres that the same source gives in its description of the wing.

| Quantity | Value | Metric |
|---|---|---|
| Length | 45 ft 0.1 in | 13.72 m |
| Wingspan, one published figure | 36 ft 0 in | 10.97 m |
| Wingspan, another published figure | 30 ft 0 in | 9.15 m |
| Height | 17 ft 3.8 in | 5.28 m |
| Wing area | 590 sq ft | 54.8 m² |
| Leading-edge sweep | 55 degrees | |
| Empty weight | 24,030 lb | 10,900 kg |
| Maximum takeoff weight | 38,000 lb | 17,200 kg |
| Engine | One [Pratt and Whitney YF119-PW-614][ref_f119] | |
| Military thrust | 28,000 lbf | 120 kN |
| Afterburning thrust | 50,000 lbf | 220 kN |
| Maximum speed | Mach 1.2 | |

### The Span Disagreement, Which Is Not a Rounding Difference

**Two published spans differ by 19.9 percent and they cannot both be right.** Thirty-six feet against thirty
is not a conversion error and not a rounding, and the two imply materially different aeroplanes.

$$A = \frac{b^{2}}{S} = \frac{36.0^{2}}{590} = 2.197 \qquad \text{against} \qquad \frac{30.02^{2}}{590} = 1.527$$

**An aspect ratio of 2.20 and one of 1.53 are different wings.** The article carries both figures rather
than choosing, because nothing available settles which is the demonstrator and which is a production
proposal, and
**a specification sheet transcribed through several publications acquires exactly this kind of error.** The
dimensional record for these aircraft is collected in [the inventory of X-vehicles][book_xvehicles], which
is also [on the reports server][ref_xvehicles].

**Where the record permits a check, it passes.** The two ratios that a specification sheet has to satisfy
are

$$\frac{W}{S} = \frac{38{,}000}{590} = 64.41 \ \text{lb/ft}^{2} \qquad \text{and} \qquad \frac{T}{W} = \frac{28{,}000}{38{,}000} = 0.737$$

with 1.316 on afterburner, and all three are ordinary values for a fighter of this size.

## Dependent Systems

### The Direct-Lift Arrangement

**A butterfly valve diverted the core stream to a pair of thrust-vectoring nozzles near the centre of gravity.**
The arrangement is a form of [thrust vectoring][ref_thrust_vectoring]. In conventional flight the exhaust
went aft through the main nozzle. In STOVL mode it went down. The arrangement is the Harrier's in principle,
executed with a modern engine and a single core rather than the Pegasus's four rotating nozzles.

**The consequence for the airframe is the chin inlet.** Putting lift nozzles at the centre of gravity puts
the engine amidships, which puts the intake forward and low, under the nose. That inlet is what most people
remember about the aeroplane, and
**it is not a styling decision but the downstream consequence of the propulsion choice**, which is the sense
in which the two entries were different aircraft rather than different arrangements.

The literature of inlets and of vectoring nozzles is gathered in the clusters on supersonic inlets, intakes and airframe-propulsion integration and on nozzles, thrust vectoring and thrust reversers under The Contemporary Literature.

### Hot Gas Ingestion, and Why It Is Worse Than It Sounds

**An aircraft hovering on its own exhaust is standing in a fountain of its own hot gas**, and some of it
reaches the inlet. The mechanism has two paths, and the aerodynamics of a jet close to a surface is the
subject of [ground effect][ref_ground_effect]. In the near field the jets impinge on the ground, spread
outward, meet under the fuselage and rise as a fountain, which
[twin-jet impingement studies][research_behrouzi_2000] treat directly. In the far field the outward wall jet
meets the free stream or the aircraft's own motion and rolls up into a ground vortex that carries hot gas
forward and up into the intake.

**The cost is thrust, and the relation is simple.** A turbofan matched at a corrected operating point
carries its mass flow and its thrust in the corrected forms

$$\dot{m}_{\text{corr}} = \frac{\dot{m} \sqrt{\theta}}{\delta} \qquad \text{and} \qquad T_{\text{corr}} = \frac{T}{\delta} \qquad \text{with} \qquad \theta = \frac{T_{t}}{T_{\text{ref}}}, \ \delta = \frac{p_{t}}{p_{\text{ref}}}$$

so at a fixed corrected point and a fixed inlet pressure the delivered thrust falls as the square root of
inlet total temperature. That is what makes reingestion expensive rather than merely untidy, and the effect
was being measured on lift engines by the end of the 1960s in
[model tests of concepts to reduce it][research_hall_1970] and correlated against
[geometric scale and surface wind][research_cosgrovewj_ryanpe_1969]. A rise of $\Delta T$ therefore costs a
fraction of thrust

$$\frac{\Delta T_{\text{thrust}}}{T} = 1 - \sqrt{\frac{T_{\text{ref}}}{T_{\text{ref}} + \Delta T}}$$

where $T_{\text{ref}}$ is the standard sea-level temperature of 59 degrees Fahrenheit, or 518.67 degrees
Rankine, and $\Delta T$ is in Fahrenheit degrees.

| Inlet temperature rise | Thrust lost |
|---|---|
| 10 degrees Fahrenheit | 0.95 percent |
| 25 degrees | 2.33 percent |
| 50 degrees | 4.50 percent |
| 100 degrees | 8.44 percent |
| 150 degrees | 11.93 percent |

The corrected-parameter treatment behind that relation is standard and is set out in
[Mattingly on engine design][book_mattingly], with the configuration consequences in
[Raymer on conceptual design][book_raymer] and the underlying aerodynamics in
[Kuethe and Chow][book_kuethe_chow].

**Those look like small numbers and they are not.** Apply the amplification derived above. A fifty degree
rise costs 4.50 percent of lift, and the allowance that remains, written $W_{\text{allowance}}'$, is

$$W_{\text{allowance}}' = \frac{L \left(1 - \Delta T_{\text{thrust}}/T\right)}{k} - W_{\text{empty}} = \frac{28{,}000 (1 - 0.0450)}{1.05} - 24{,}030 = 1{,}437 \ \text{lb}$$

so the fraction of the allowance the rise consumes is the amplification times the thrust lost,

$$\frac{W_{\text{allowance}} - W_{\text{allowance}}'}{W_{\text{allowance}}} = \frac{L/k}{W_{\text{allowance}}} \, \frac{\Delta T_{\text{thrust}}}{T} = 10.11 \times 0.0450 = 0.455$$

which is **45.5 percent of the entire bring-back allowance**, taking it from 2,637 pounds to 1,437. The same
relation applied to the 2.33 percent lost to a twenty-five degree rise gives 23.5 percent.

**The ingestion penalty comes off the small number, not the large one**, and that is the whole difficulty
stated in one sentence.

### The Feedback, Which Is What Makes It Dangerous

**A thrust deficit at hover is not a performance shortfall. It is a descent.** Lift below weight gives a
downward acceleration of

$$a = g \, \frac{\Delta T_{\text{thrust}}}{T}$$

which for a fifty degree rise is **1.45 feet per second squared**. Integrating twice gives the sink,

$$s = \tfrac{1}{2} a t^{2} = \tfrac{1}{2} (1.45)(2)^{2} = 2.89 \ \text{ft in two seconds}$$

against 1.50 feet for a twenty-five degree rise.

**And a descent is proximity to the ground, and proximity is more ingestion.** The loop closes on itself.
The pilot's response is more throttle, which produces more hot gas, which is fed back into the inlet.

**This is why the difficulty is not measured by the temperature rise alone.** A system with margin absorbs
it. A system operating where its allowance is badly conditioned does not.

The experimental and computational record of hot gas ingestion and of the jet-induced ground environment is gathered in the clusters on hot gas ingestion, reingestion and inlet temperature distortion and on jet-induced ground effects, suckdown, fountains and ground vortices under The Contemporary Literature.

### What the Lift Fan Does About the Same Problem

**The lift fan attacks hot gas ingestion three times over, and none of the three was designed for that purpose.**
They are consequences of the square-root law.

**The exhaust is cooler**, and the mixing relation says by how much. The parameters that correlate ingestion
were identified early, in [Krishnamoorthy 1971][research_krishnamoorthyv_1971]. Two
streams combine at the mass-weighted mean of their temperatures,

$$T_{\text{mix}} = \frac{\dot{m}_c T_c + \dot{m}_h T_h}{\dot{m}_c + \dot{m}_h}$$

and the mass flows follow from the thrusts and the velocities, since $\dot{m} = T / V$.

**Here the square-root law appears from the other side, and it is worth pausing on.** The cold stream is
slower, so it carries **more mass per unit of thrust** than the hot one. At a 47.7 percent share of the lift
it carries **60.9 percent of the mass**.

| Cold share of thrust | Cold share of mass | Mixed-mean exhaust |
|---|---|---|
| 0 percent, all direct lift | 0 percent | 1,200 degrees Fahrenheit |
| 30 percent | 42.3 percent | 718 degrees |
| **47.7 percent, the lift system** | **60.9 percent** | **505 degrees** |
| 60 percent | 72.0 percent | 379 degrees |

**Roughly seven hundred degrees of exhaust temperature is bought by an architecture chosen for thrust per unit of power**,
and the temperatures above use a representative core exit value rather than a published one, so the column
is indicative and the trend is not.

**The exhaust is also slower**, because the disc loading is lower, and the ground vortex is driven by the
wall jet's momentum. **And the cold stream is in front**, since the fan sits behind the cockpit and the hot
core exhausts at the tail, so the cold jet stands between the hot one and the intake.

**A design chosen for thrust per unit power turns out to solve the ingestion problem as a side effect.**
That is the sense in which the competition was decided by a relation rather than by a contest.

### Hover Control and the Reaction Controls

**An aircraft in the hover has no aerodynamic controls**, because control surfaces need dynamic pressure and
there is none. Attitude has to be held by differential thrust.

The lift-fan aircraft used roll posts in the wings, contributing 3,900 pounds of lift between them and
providing roll control by differential throttling, with pitch from the balance between the fan and the
swivel module and yaw from the swivel module's lateral deflection.

The moment available from a pair of posts is the differential thrust on the lateral arm,

$$M = \Delta T \, y$$

so posts carrying 3,900 pounds between them at a station ten feet outboard command up to 39,000 foot-pounds
of roll moment at full differential.

**The direct-lift aircraft had a harder problem** because all of its lift came from one place. Roll control
requires either bleed-air reaction jets or differential nozzle modulation, and the first of those has a
price that is paid before any moment is produced.
**Bleeding a fraction $b$ of the mass flow removes that fraction of the thrust**,

$$L_{\text{after bleed}} = (1 - b) L$$

and the loss comes off the allowance with the amplification derived earlier.

| Bleed fraction | Lift lost | Allowance remaining |
|---|---|---|
| 0 percent | 0 lb | 2,637 lb |
| 2 percent | 560 lb | 2,103 lb |
| 4 percent | 1,120 lb | 1,570 lb |
| 6 percent | 1,680 lb | 1,037 lb |

**A four percent bleed costs forty percent of everything the aircraft can bring home, and buys a roll control that has not yet been used to do anything.**
The lift-fan aircraft takes its roll control from posts that are already producing lift, so the same
function costs it nothing it was not already spending.

The literature of hover control and reaction controls is gathered in the cluster on handling qualities, flight control and hover control under The Contemporary Literature.

### The Wing, and the Change That Matters Most

**Boeing's demonstrators flew a large single-piece composite delta with [55 degrees of leading-edge sweep][ref_x32] and no horizontal tail.**
The production proposal did not use that wing. It used a conventional wing with a canted twin tail, and the
change was made **before the X-32 ever flew**.

**That fact is the most important one in this article and it is not a propulsion fact.**

A concept demonstrator exists to reduce the risk of a proposal. If the demonstrator does not carry the
proposal's configuration in the feature under examination, then flying it establishes something about a
different aeroplane.
**The X-32 demonstrated that a delta-winged direct-lift aircraft could hover and go supersonic. Boeing was offering a tailed aircraft.**

**The customer was therefore looking at an aeroplane nobody intended to build**, and had to reason from it
to one that did not exist, while the competitor's demonstrator was configurationally much closer to its own
proposal.

## The Flight Test Record

**The two aircraft flew 144 times between them**, with the X-32A opening on 18 September 2000 and the X-32B
on 29 March 2001, and the flight programme ending before the decision in October 2001.

| Event | Date |
|---|---|
| Concept demonstration contracts awarded | November 1996 |
| X-32A first flight | 18 September 2000 |
| X-32B first flight | 29 March 2001 |
| X-35B first short takeoff, supersonic dash and vertical landing in one flight | 20 July 2001 |
| Decision announced | 26 October 2001 |

**The X-32A demonstrated conventional flight, supersonic performance and air refuelling.** The X-32B
demonstrated hover and vertical landing. **What neither demonstrated was both at once**, because the
aircraft had to be physically reconfigured between the two roles.

### The Reconfiguration, Stated Precisely

**This is where the article has to be careful, because the popular account overstates it and the underlying fact is damaging enough without help.**

The X-32 was a single-engine aircraft whose STOVL system diverted the core exhaust. Converting the
demonstrator between the conventional and the STOVL configurations was a ground operation, not a cockpit
switch.
**The aircraft could not take off short, accelerate through the sound barrier and land vertically on the same sortie**,
and it did not attempt to.

**The X-35B did exactly that on 20 July 2001.** A short takeoff, a level supersonic dash and a vertical
landing, in one flight.

**The evidentiary difference is not that one aircraft is faster or lifts more.** It is that one of them
demonstrated the **integration** and the other demonstrated the **components**. A customer buying an
aircraft that must do all three in an operational sortie has been shown the thing itself in one case and its
parts in the other.

### What the X-32B Did Not Do

**By accounts from the test community the X-32B never performed a vertical landing at full weight.** This
article treats that as reported rather than established, because the sources available for it are secondary,
and marks it accordingly in the Epistemic State.

**But the arithmetic above says what such a report would mean if true**, and it is not a qualitative
shortcoming. At an assumed 28,000 pounds of lift, the bring-back relation displayed in the section headed
The Number the Competition Was Actually About gives an allowance over empty weight of 2,637 pounds before any
ingestion penalty, and the break-even relation displayed in the section that follows it puts the lift at
which the allowance vanishes entirely at **25,232 pounds**. A
demonstrator flying with instrumentation, ballast and test equipment aboard is not at its empty weight.

**The jet screen fitted to hold hot gas away from the intake made the arithmetic worse rather than better**,
because it added weight and bled air from the engine, and both come off the same small number.

## Comparison With Ground Prediction

### The Prediction That Mattered Was Not Aerodynamic

**In most articles in this series this section compares a wind tunnel against flight.** Here the comparison
that decided the outcome was between a **prediction about a production aircraft** and the evidence a
demonstrator could supply, and the two are not commensurable.

**The ground prediction for hot gas ingestion was, and remains, difficult.** The phenomenon involves an
impinging jet, a wall jet, a fountain, a ground vortex and an inlet, at full-scale temperatures, in a flow
field that depends on the height above the ground and on any wind. Scale models mispredict it because the
temperature ratios and the Reynolds numbers do not scale together.

**The NASA research base on this is substantial and predates the competition by two decades**, and it is the
reason the physics was understood well enough that neither company was surprised in principle. What could
not be settled on the ground was the margin. That research base is gathered in the cluster on jet-induced ground effects, suckdown, fountains and ground vortices under The Contemporary Literature, where 85 of its 143 records date from 1970 to 1995.

### Where the Two Demonstrators Sat Relative to Their Proposals

| | Boeing | Lockheed Martin |
|---|---|---|
| Demonstrator wing | Large delta, no horizontal tail | Conventional wing and tail |
| Proposed wing | Conventional wing, canted twin tail | Conventional wing and tail |
| STOVL and supersonic in one sortie | No, reconfiguration required | Yes, demonstrated 20 July 2001 |
| Lift architecture demonstrated | The proposed one | The proposed one |

**Both companies demonstrated their propulsion architecture and only one demonstrated its airframe.** That
asymmetry is the answer to what the fly-off decided, and it is an answer about evidence rather than about
aeroplanes.

The acquisition literature on prototypes and proposals is gathered in the cluster on defence acquisition, competition, prototyping and cost under The Contemporary Literature.

## What the Data Changed

### The Winner Acquired a Literature and the Loser Did Not

**This article's second finding concerns the literature itself, and it is measurable.**

The reference set this article cites holds 3,528 research records from the NASA reports server, the Defense
Technical Information Center and the Crossref registry. Counting the titles of those records that contain
each term gives the following, and anyone can repeat the count on the cited set.

| Term in the title | Records |
|---|---|
| **X-32** | **1** |
| X-35 | 6 |
| F-35 | 38 |
| Joint Strike Fighter | 72 |
| Harrier or Pegasus | 46 |

**Exactly one of those 3,528 records carries the X-32 in its title, and it was written by the engine supplier rather than the airframer.**
It reports testing of the Rolls-Royce lift system for the X-32B, in 2002, after the competition was over.

**The winner's record runs from 2002 to 2026.** The loser's stops at one. Writing the asymmetry
as a ratio,

$$R = \frac{N_{\text{winner}}}{N_{\text{loser}}} = \frac{38}{1} = 38$$

against two aircraft that flew in the same years, under the same programme, for the same customer.

**That is not a claim that Boeing did no engineering.** It is a claim about what becomes public. An aircraft
that enters production is written about for twenty years by the people who build it, operate it, modify it
and study it. An aircraft that loses a competition is written about by nobody, because its proprietary data
stays proprietary and there is no operating community to generate questions.

**The consequence for this article is uncomfortable and is stated rather than hidden.** The technical case
made here rests on published numbers for the winning system and on an assumed upper bound for the losing
one, because the losing one's numbers were never published.
**The asymmetry in the evidence available to a historian is a direct inheritance of the asymmetry in the decision.**

The X-32, X-35, F-35 and Joint Strike Fighter titles counted here are gathered in the cluster on the Joint Strike Fighter programme and its demonstrators under The Contemporary Literature.

### The Architecture Won and Kept Winning

**The shaft-driven lift fan is now the only supersonic STOVL system in production anywhere**, and the
arrangement demonstrated in 2001 is the arrangement flying on the [F-35][ref_f35] today. Its designers
received the [Collier Trophy][ref_collier] for 2001.

**The direct-lift approach did not fail. It stopped being competitive**, which is a different statement. The
Harrier flew for decades on direct lift and did useful work, and the reason it was not the answer for a
supersonic aircraft is the same square-root law, applied to a heavier aeroplane where the allowance runs
out.

### What the Competition Format Itself Established

**The concept demonstration phase is now a reference case in [acquisition literature][research_fast_2014]**,
a field whose institutional history is set out in [Gansler on the defence industry][book_gansler] and whose
cost-growth folklore is collected in [Augustine's laws][book_augustine], cited both as evidence that
competitive prototyping produces better decisions and as evidence that it produces expensive ones. The
programme that followed acquired a reputation for cost growth that the fly-off did not prevent.

**That is worth stating plainly because it cuts against the natural reading of this article.** The fly-off
made a defensible technical choice, on the best evidence available, and the resulting programme still
exceeded its estimates by a wide margin.
**A good decision procedure on the propulsion question did not produce a good outcome on cost**, because the
two questions were not the same question.

The competitive-prototyping literature is gathered in the cluster on defence acquisition, competition, prototyping and cost under The Contemporary Literature.

## The Contemporary Literature

| Cluster | Records |

| The Joint Strike Fighter programme and its demonstrators | 118 |
| The Harrier, the Pegasus engine and the vectored-thrust lineage | 85 |
| Hot gas ingestion, reingestion and inlet temperature distortion | 76 |
| Jet-induced ground effects, suckdown, fountains and ground vortices | 143 |
| Jet impingement and jet flow physics | 120 |
| Ship decks, landing surfaces and shipboard operations | 117 |
| Lift fans, lift engines, ejectors and remote lift | 220 |
| Nozzles, thrust vectoring and thrust reversers | 419 |
| Momentum theory, rotors, propellers and hover performance | 213 |
| Ducted fans and ducted-fan vehicles | 117 |
| Distributed and electric propulsion and electric vertical flight | 156 |
| Supersonic inlets, intakes and airframe-propulsion integration | 243 |
| Engine cycles, variable cycle engines and engine performance | 278 |
| Defence acquisition, competition, prototyping and cost | 280 |
| Commonality, product families and requirements | 88 |
| Decision analysis, selection and cost-effectiveness | 47 |
| Digital engineering, model-based systems engineering and machine learning | 87 |
| Low observability and signatures | 32 |
| Automatic and autonomous landing and guidance | 18 |
| Handling qualities, flight control and hover control | 235 |
| V/STOL and STOVL aircraft concepts, programmes and design studies | 267 |
| Flight test, instrumentation and system identification | 14 |
| Fighter and supersonic aircraft design | 104 |
| High-lift and powered-lift aerodynamics | 51 |
| **Total** | **3,528** |

**The X-32 flew before three of the fields that now own its questions existed**, and one of those fields
exists because of the aircraft that beat it.

### Powered lift became an electric problem

**The square-root law is the reason the electric vertical-takeoff industry looks the way it does.** Every
configuration in that field spreads its lift over as much disc area as the airframe will carry, because the
power required for a given lift rises as the square root of the disc loading and battery energy is the
binding constraint.
**The design pressure that produced the lift fan produces distributed rotors when the power source is a battery**,
and it is the same relation. Distributed electric propulsion is reviewed in [Kim et al 2018][research_kim_perry_2018], and [Fulton and Gandhi 2026][research_fulton_gandhi_2026] treat the disc loading of multi-passenger rotors as a design variable.

### Hot gas and jet impingement are computed now

**The flow the competition could not settle on the ground is now simulated**, and the modern literature on
impinging jets, ground vortices and thermal fields around vertical-landing aircraft is substantial. The
difficulty that remains is the one that was always there, namely that the temperature ratios and the
Reynolds numbers do not scale together. Fountain formation between twin impinging jets is computed in [Zhang and Agarwal 2020][research_zhang_agarwal_2020], and the heat transfer of an impinging round jet by large eddy simulation in [Li et al 2016][research_li_zhang_2016].

### Deck and surface heating became an operational subject

**A supersonic aircraft landing vertically puts a hot, fast jet onto a surface**, and the surface has to
survive it. That is a materials and operations subject that the Harrier introduced and the production
successor made acute, and it is a direct descendant of the disc-loading argument in this article.

**The identity derived earlier makes the connection exact rather than rhetorical.** The disc loading is the
dynamic pressure in the jet, so an architecture at 2,852 pounds per square foot imposes almost twice the
pressure of one at 1,467, on the same deck, at the same landing weight.
**Ships were resurfaced because of that number**, and the choice that produced it was made on propulsion
grounds with the deck consequence following. Airfield concrete under the F-35B is studied in [Autelitano et al 2016][research_autelitano_iacci_2016] and [Balaban and Erdem 2022][research_balaban_erdem_2022], a flight deck heat spreader in [Semenic et al 2016][research_semenic_gould_2016], and exhaust gases and over-deck air temperatures on a ship in [Scott et al 2015][research_scott_owen_2015].

### The fly-off was partly replaced by simulation

**The question this article asks is what evidence a competition can gather about an aircraft that does not yet exist, and the modern answer is that some of it need not be gathered by building two aeroplanes.**

Digital engineering, model-based systems engineering and simulation-based acquisition are the literature of
that shift. A model does not have the X-32's difficulty, which was that the demonstrator differed from the
proposal in the feature under examination, because a model can be made to be the proposal.

**What a model does not have is the thing the X-35B's single sortie demonstrated**, namely that the
integration works in the presence of everything nobody modelled.
**The trade is between an evidence source that matches the proposal exactly and one that matches reality exactly**,
and this competition is the case that shows why neither alone is sufficient. The digital thread in defence acquisition is examined in [West and Pyster 2015][research_west_pyster_2015], and the adoption of virtual prototyping across the defence research and acquisition communities in [Post and Sundt 2017][research_post_sundt_2017].

### Acquisition analysis kept the case

**The concept demonstration phase remains a case in the literature on competitive prototyping.** Competition within the programme itself is examined in [Assessing Competitive Strategies for the Joint Strike Fighter: Opportunities and Options 2001][research_assessing_competitive_2001] and [Birkler 2001][research_birkler_2001], and prototyping more widely in [Cate 1997][research_cate_1997], [Medlej et al 2017][research_medlej_stuban_2017] and [Fast 2014][research_fast_2014].

### Decision analysis under uncertainty

**The question this article calls construct validity has a formal literature.** Choosing between
alternatives on evidence that does not directly measure the quantity of interest is the subject of decision
analysis. Its literature includes [Montibeller and von Winterfeldt 2015][research_montibeller_vonwinterfeldt_2015] on biases in multi-criteria analysis, [French 2025][research_french_2025] on scenario-focused decision analysis and [Kahneman and Tversky 1977][research_kahneman_tversky_1977] on decisions under risk.

### Propulsion, nozzles and integration since

**The direct-lift arrangement made the propulsion system the centre of the competition, and its components are still being studied.** The X-32B diverted its core stream through a butterfly valve to a pair of thrust-vectoring nozzles near the centre of gravity, which is what forced the chin inlet, and the winner added a lift fan, a three-bearing swivel module and roll posts. The modern work on those components and their neighbours, namely fluidic and mechanical thrust vectoring, swivel nozzles and thrust reversers, supersonic inlets and their bleed systems, and the coupling of engine and airframe performance, is gathered in the clusters on nozzles, thrust vectoring and thrust reversers and on supersonic inlets, intakes and airframe-propulsion integration below. The three-bearing swivel duct is modelled and controlled in [Wei et al 2019][research_wei_tian_2019] and designed in [Shi et al 2026][research_shi_zhou_2026], and a ventral diverterless inlet is analysed in [Ge et al 2021][research_ge_shang_2021].

### Autonomy arrived in the hover

**The precision required for a vertical landing on a moving deck is now within reach of automatic systems**,
which changes what the pilot has to do and therefore what the control power has to cover. Autonomous ship-deck landing is treated in [Grocholsky et al 2016][research_grocholsky_defranco_2016] and [Lee et al 2021][research_lee_benedict_2021], and the precision hover control of the production aircraft in [Denham and Paines 2008][research_denham_paines_2008].

### Machine learning and digital engineering

**The fly-off was partly replaced by simulation, and the data-driven tools those simulations are now built from are gathered in the cluster on digital engineering, model-based systems engineering and machine learning below.** The article argues that a model can be made to be the proposal but does not match reality exactly, which is why the X-35B's single sortie still mattered. The records there apply digital threads and twins, model-based systems engineering, neural networks and reinforcement learning to aircraft, engines and their design, and they extend the subject of simulation-based acquisition rather than replace it. [Massegur Sampietro and Da Ronch 2024][research_massegursampietro_daronch_2024] apply geometric deep learning to the aerodynamics of a future fighter demonstrator in dynamic manoeuvres.

### Momentum theory, still

**The relation this article's sizing rests on has been in the textbooks since momentum theory was written, and it is still being refined.** Momentum theory relates disc loading to induced velocity, and through it the power required for a given lift rises as the square root of the disc loading, which is the steep curve that separates the lift fan from a vectoring nozzle. The cluster on momentum theory, rotors, propellers and hover performance below holds the work that extends actuator-disc and blade-element momentum theory to open rotors, propellers and hovering rotors and, as neighbouring science, to tidal and wind turbines, among them [Mahmuddin 2017][research_mahmuddin_2017] on blade element momentum theory and [Shives and Crawford 2017][research_shives_crawford_2017] on a tuned actuator disc for tidal turbines.

### Low observability

**This literature is thin, with 32 records in the cluster on low observability and signatures below.** The role of radar cross section in stealth design is set out in [Lee 2024][research_lee_2024], the radar cross section of engine inlets including their fan blades is computed in [Vogel 2016][research_vogel_2016], and a low-observable intake is designed in [Edefur and Tormalm 2018][research_edefur_tormalm_2018]. Low-observable design shaped both entries
decisively and is almost entirely absent from the open literature, which is what one should expect of the
subject.

### The Harrier, the Pegasus engine and the vectored-thrust lineage

### Defence acquisition, competition, prototyping and cost

Defence acquisition, competitive prototyping and fly-offs, source selection, cost estimation and cost growth, acquisition reform, and operational test and evaluation as part of acquisition.

**140 of these 280 records are report primaries, the highest share of any cluster**, because the literature of the decision is mostly written by the institutions that make such decisions.

## Where the Framing Breaks Down

**Treating this aircraft through its keystone misleads in five places.**

### The Square-Root Law Is Not the Whole Trade

**A lift fan costs weight, volume and complexity, and this article has priced none of them.** The fan, its
shaft, its clutch and its gearbox occupy the space immediately behind the cockpit in all three variants,
including the two that never use them, and every pound of that hardware is carried on every sortie by every
aircraft in the fleet.

**A direct-lift system's whole appeal is that it adds almost nothing.** The engine is already there and the
valve and the nozzles are comparatively light.
**The correct comparison is not thrust against thrust but thrust against the weight and volume spent to obtain it**,
and this article has computed only one side of it.

### The Bring-Back Number Uses an Assumed Lift

**The X-32B's STOVL lift is not published and the article assumes the engine's military rating as an upper bound.**
Every allowance figure inherits that assumption. The sensitivity table shows that the conclusion holds
across the plausible range and that no value inside it produces a comfortable allowance, but a published
figure could move the numbers and the article would have to follow.

### A Fly-Off Is Not the Only Evidence

**The decision was not made by watching two aeroplanes fly.** It rested on proposals, cost estimates,
manufacturing plans, supportability analyses and technical data packages, of which the flight demonstrations
were one input.
**Treating the competition as a race is a simplification that this article has adopted for the sake of a clean argument**,
and the acquisition literature is explicit that source selection weighs many factors of which demonstrated
performance is one.

### The Wing Change Cuts Both Ways

**The article treats Boeing's mid-competition wing change as damaging to its evidentiary position, and it can be read the opposite way.**
A company that identifies a configuration problem and fixes it before proposing has done exactly what a
risk-reduction phase is for.
**The customer could reasonably have credited the correction rather than penalising the mismatch**, and the
public record does not say which reading the source selection took.

### The Aircraft Was Not Bad

**It flew 144 times, went supersonic, refuelled in the air, hovered and landed vertically.** The pilots who
flew it have consistently said it handled well.
**An aeroplane that does everything asked of it and loses is not a failure of engineering**, and an article
organised around why it lost risks implying that it should not have been built.

## The Source Base

**The research survey holds 3,528 research records drawn from Crossref, the NASA Technical Reports Server and the Defense Technical Information Center**, of which 629 are report primaries, being 17.8 percent, by the identifier rule set out under The Definition of Primary. **The median publication year is 2003.** Of the 3,500 dated records, 1,747 were published through 2002 and 1,480 from 2015 onward, and the table gives each period as a share of the dated records.
**Every anchored record is cited.**

| Period | Records | Share |
|---|---|---|
| Before 1970 | 287 | 8.2 percent |
| 1970 to 1984 | 663 | 18.9 percent |
| 1985 to 1995 | 501 | 14.3 percent |
| **1996 to 2002, the programme window** | **296** | **8.5 percent** |
| 2003 to 2014 | 273 | 7.8 percent |
| 2015 onward | 1,480 | 42.3 percent |
| of which 2022 onward | 730 | 20.9 percent |

**The count and the fraction are reported together**, because adding a contemporary survey holds the period
count and lowers its fraction while adding period sources does the reverse. Neither movement is a fact about
coverage.

### The Equations That Carry No Citation

**Some displayed relations carry no citation of their own and that is deliberate.** They are the relations
that construct the bring-back allowance and follow its consequences, the roll moment as differential thrust
times a lateral arm, and the ratio between the winner's and the loser's record counts.

**The bring-back relations and the record-count ratio are constructions original to this article**, so
attaching a citation would be false attribution. The weights and thrusts they take as inputs are cited where
they are first given. **The roll moment is elementary statics** and citing it would be worse than leaving it
bare.

**Everything else carries its literature.** Momentum theory, the figure of merit, the disc loading as a
dynamic pressure, the corrected engine parameters, the ingestion correlations, the twin-jet fountain and the
competitive-prototyping case all rest on published work and say whose.

### This Article Has Two Primary Bases and They Sit in Different Decades

**A primary source is one contemporary with the work, and for this article the work is two things that happened thirty years apart.**

**The decision is a 1996 to 2002 subject.** Source selection, competitive prototyping, cost estimation and
requirements commonality are primary in that window and nowhere else, and that band holds 296 records.

**The physics is not.** The short takeoff and vertical landing research the competition rested on was done
in the 1970s and 1980s, largely by NASA, and
**a 1985 report on hot gas ingestion is a primary source for this article's central failure mechanism in a way that a 2019 review is not**.
That base holds **1,164 records across 1970 to 1995** and is the healthier of the two.

**Treating the whole of the pre-2003 material as one undifferentiated period band would hide that distinction**,
so the table above separates them.

### Two Contemporary Headings Follow From the Argument

**Two contemporary subjects follow from results this article derives rather than from being merely recent.**

**Shipboard thermal and deck interaction is the operational consequence of the disc-loading identity derived in the sizing section.**
An architecture at 2,852 pounds per square foot imposes almost twice the pressure of one at 1,467 on the
same deck at the same landing weight, and **ships were resurfaced because of that number**.

**Digital engineering and model-based acquisition is what partly replaced the fly-off.** A model does not
have the X-32's difficulty, since a model can be made to be the proposal, but a model also does not have
what the X-35B's single sortie demonstrated.
**The trade is between an evidence source that matches the proposal exactly and one that matches reality exactly**,
and this competition is the case that shows why neither alone is sufficient.

### A Homonym Inside the Discipline

**Hot gas ingestion is also a turbomachinery subject, and it uses the identical phrase.** Sealing flows
between a turbine rotor disc and its stator are an active field whose papers are titled "hot-gas ingestion"
exactly as the inlet problem is.

**Forty-four cited research titles contain the words "hot gas", and none concerns a turbine rim.**
Rim cavities, purge flows and sealing flows in turbines, and combustor dampers, are excluded. Dust, particle
and salt ingestion are a different inlet problem and are excluded too, and no cited title names
any of them.

**That is the most dangerous class of homonym, because it is internal to the discipline.**
**Bird ingestion is deliberately admitted**, because an inlet swallowing a bird and an inlet swallowing its
own exhaust are the same fluid mechanics.

### The Hot Gas Ingestion Literature Is Thin

**Sixty-one cited research records, each under its own title, concern the ingestion, reingestion, recirculation or environment of hot or exhaust gas.**
The open literature on inlet reingestion is genuinely small, because the measurements that
matter are full-scale, expensive, and made by manufacturers rather than by research agencies.
**The article's central failure mechanism rests on a thin public base and says so.**

### The Vehicle Cluster Is the Finding

**One cited research record carries the X-32 in its title.** It comes from the engine supplier and was
published after the decision.

**That is reported as a finding rather than as a gap**, and the article's own argument depends on saying so,
because it is the reason the technical case here rests on the winner's published numbers and the loser's
inferred bound.

### The Literature of the Decision

**The keystone of this article is not a physical quantity, so part of its literature is the literature of a decision.**
Source selection, competitive prototyping, cost growth and requirements commonality are that literature, and
they have their own headings because a paper on acquisition strategy is not a paper on aerodynamics.

**The keystone word is also the worst homonym.** Competition between firms and competition between species
are both enormous literatures, and both dwarf the procurement sense. So do dietary ingestion against inlet
ingestion, and language acquisition against defence acquisition. Records in those other senses are
excluded.

### The Definition of Primary

**Primacy is a property of the document, and this survey derives it from the identifier.** A record is a report primary when its address is on the NASA Technical Reports Server or it carries a digital object identifier under the 10.21236 prefix, which Crossref registers for Defense Technical Information Center reports. **The NASA reports on powered lift of the 1970s and 1980s that carry this article's physics are report primaries under that rule**, and so are 140 of the 280 acquisition studies. **The rule does not reach the conference and journal papers of the competition years**, such as the engine supplier's report on the X-32B lift system and the X-35B control papers of 2002, so 17.8 percent is a lower bound on how much of the survey is first-hand.

### Thin Subjects

**Seven subjects that this article's derivations and conclusions depend on have thin literatures.** Each count below is the number of research records in this survey whose title carries at least one of the listed terms, with a hyphen read as a space.

| Subject | Title terms counted | Records |
|---|---|---|
| the bring-back allowance in vertical landing | bring back, bringback, vertical landing weight, vertical landing thrust | 1 |
| disc loading as the variable of the square-root law | disc loading, disk loading | 13 |
| the fountain between two lifting jets | fountain | 14 |
| suckdown in hover | suckdown, suck down | 10 |
| the three-bearing swivel nozzle | swivel, three bearing, 3 bearing | 10 |
| source selection between competing designs | source selection | 10 |
| construct validity of a demonstration | construct validity | 0 |

**The bring-back allowance has one title.** It concerns the vertical landing thrust requirements of a STOVL aircraft, so the allowance relations derived above are this article's own construction, as the subsection on uncited equations says.

**Disc loading is named in 13 titles, and 12 of them name a low or a high disc loading.** Those titles compare configurations by their disc loading rather than derive the square-root law, which is textbook momentum theory.

**The fountain has 14 titles, of which 8 date from before 1995**, and suckdown has 10, so the two jet-induced effects the hover argument names are carried by a small and mostly older literature.

**The three-bearing swivel nozzle that the winner flew has 10 titles, and 8 of them date from 2014 or later**, after the nozzle had flown on the production aircraft.

**Source selection has 10 titles and none of them names an aircraft.** The account of how the competition was decided therefore rests on the programme's public record rather than on a published analysis of this source selection.

**No title names construct validity.** The term is used here in its sense from measurement theory, as set out in [construct validity][ref_construct_validity].

### What the Survey Admits

**A record is admitted only when a person reading its title finds it on this article's subject.** That subject is the short takeoff and vertical landing fighter and the decision between two of them. A title is therefore admitted when it concerns lift propulsion by direct lift, lift fans, lift engines or ejectors, momentum theory and hover performance, jet-induced and ground effects, hot gas ingestion and inlet temperature distortion, nozzles and thrust vectoring, impinging and hot jets, ship decks and landing surfaces, or the design, control and history of vertical and short takeoff aircraft. It is admitted too when it concerns fighter design, inlets and fighter engine cycles, low observability, carrier approach and landing, electric vertical flight and distributed propulsion for lift, or the acquisition, commonality, decision analysis and digital engineering of a competition between prototypes. A title that shares only general aeronautics with that subject is excluded. So are the classes of title that most often do so, namely turbomachinery components and engine health, conventional electric, hybrid, hydrogen and solar aircraft, the flight control and structures of helicopters, the flight control and handling of conventional fixed-wing aircraft, small unmanned aircraft and their autonomy, flight test of unrelated aircraft, generic aerodynamics and unrelated airframe subsystems, hypersonic vehicles, supersonic transports and air cushion vehicles, and decision and digital methods applied to systems other than aircraft. Many words this subject depends on also name something else, and a record that shares only such a word is excluded. Figure of merit also names measures in electronics, optics and thermoelectric materials. Lift also names gas lift in oil wells, heavy lift in shipping and the lift of hydrofoils, and thrust also names the thrust bearings of rotating machinery. Hot gas ingestion also names the sealing flows at turbine rims, inlet temperature distortion also names the hot streaks a combustor sends into a turbine, and reingestion also names a feeding behaviour of rabbits, hares and gophers. Landing pads are also built on the Moon and on Mars, and nozzles are also rocket nozzles, so aerospike, pintle, solid-motor and spacecraft nozzles and reaction controls are excluded. The designations F119 and F135 also number abstracts in medical conference programmes. Propulsion research on ships, underwater vehicles and spacecraft, and acquisition in its data, visual, seismic and corporate senses, share the vocabulary of this subject without its subject, and so does impingement by liquid jets. Correction, erratum, retraction and withdrawal notices, journal front matter and conference indexes are excluded as well, because a survey counts research works, and one document deposited twice is counted once.

**Some records of neighbouring science are kept as doubtful.** Momentum theory for tidal, wind and hydrokinetic turbines shares the sizing relation, industrial cooling by impinging air jets shares the physics of the hot jet on a deck, and product-family commonality and technology readiness assessment outside aircraft share the management side of the decision. Each is kept, and a reader may weigh it accordingly.

**Every research title has been read for relevance, so the off-topic share that remains is a matter of reading judgement.** A reading of titles cannot show that a work is relevant in substance, so this is a statement about titles only.

**The survey has no residual cluster.** Every admitted title belongs to one of the named literatures above. Titles on test and evaluation belong to the acquisition literature, and analyses of takeoff, landing and flight performance belong to flight test, so a title whose only link to the subject is a test, evaluation or simulation vocabulary is excluded.

## Epistemic State

**Historical fact, from the published record.**

- Two X-32 demonstrators were built by Boeing under a concept demonstration contract awarded in
  November 1996. The X-32A first flew on 18 September 2000 and the X-32B on 29 March 2001.
- The two aircraft flew 144 times between them.
- The X-32B used direct lift, diverting the core exhaust to vectoring nozzles near the centre of
  gravity, and required ground reconfiguration between the conventional and STOVL arrangements.
- The X-35B performed a short takeoff, a level supersonic dash and a vertical landing in a single
  flight on 20 July 2001.
- The Department of Defense selected the Lockheed Martin entry on 26 October 2001.
- The Rolls-Royce lift system produces 20,000 pounds from the lift fan, 18,000 from the swivel
  module and 3,900 from the roll posts, for a published total of 41,900 pounds, with 29,000 shaft
  horsepower delivered to a fan 50 inches in diameter.
- Boeing's production proposal used a conventional wing with a canted twin tail rather than the
  demonstrators' delta, and the change was made before the X-32 flew.

**Engineering analysis performed for this article and verified independently.**

- Thrust for a given power rises as the square root of mass flow. This is an identity, obtained by
  eliminating the jet velocity between the thrust and power relations.
- The lift fan's published diameter, thrust and shaft power imply a figure of merit of 0.697, which
  is a value a real ducted fan can achieve, so the three numbers are mutually consistent.
- Matching that disc loading with 28,000 pounds of core thrust would need 19.09 square feet of
  nozzle, a single opening 4.93 feet across, which does not fit on the airframe.
- A fifty degree Fahrenheit inlet temperature rise costs 4.50 percent of thrust.
- At an empty weight of 24,030 pounds and a five percent margin, the direct-lift allowance over
  empty weight is 2,637 pounds against 15,875 for the lift system, a ratio of 6.02.
- The allowance amplifies a fractional thrust change by 10.11 for direct lift and 2.51 for the lift
  system, so the same physical loss costs the direct-lift aircraft four times as much.
- The direct-lift allowance vanishes entirely below 25,232 pounds of lift.
- One record in the cited set of 3,528 carries the X-32 in its title, against 38 for the F-35.

**Inference, where the record supports a conclusion without stating it.**

- **The X-32B's STOVL lift is not published**, and this article uses the engine's military rating as
  an upper bound. Every allowance figure inherits that assumption, and the sensitivity table is
  given so the reader can see how much rests on it.
- The nozzle sizes in the disc-loading table are plausible values rather than published ones, used
  to bracket the comparison.
- The attribution of the chin inlet to the propulsion choice is an inference from the geometry of
  where lift nozzles must sit, and the sources describe the arrangement without drawing that
  conclusion.

**Claims the record does not settle.**

- **The X-32B may never have landed vertically at full weight.** That is reported by the test
  community rather than established here, and the sources available for it are secondary.
- **The lift fan's weight, volume and cost penalty is not computed here.** The trade is therefore
  presented from one side only.
- **The source selection's reasoning is not public in any detail.** The weight it gave to the
  single-sortie demonstration, to the wing change, or to cost cannot be established.
- Two published wingspans differ by 19.9 percent and nothing available resolves which applies to
  the demonstrator.
- Whether Boeing's wing change should be read as a weakness in its evidentiary position or as
  evidence of a working risk-reduction process is a judgement the record does not settle.

## Out of Scope

- The F-35 programme after 2001, including its cost history, its variants and its service entry.
- The detailed design of the Rolls-Royce lift system beyond the published thrust and power figures.
- Low-observable design beyond the thin cluster in The Contemporary Literature, since it shaped both entries decisively but is almost absent from the open literature.
- The McDonnell Douglas entry eliminated in 1996, which used a gas-coupled lift fan and deserves
  separate treatment.
- Carrier suitability and the Navy variant's structural requirements.
- The international partnership arrangements that funded part of the programme.

## Conclusion

**The X-32 lost a competition it was well built to enter, and the physics of why is a single relation that has been in the textbooks for a century.**

Thrust for a given power rises as the square root of mass flow. A lift fan spends its power on a large cold
stream and a direct-lift system spends the same power on a smaller hot one, so the first produces more lift
from the same core. **Everything that separated the two aircraft follows from that.** The cooler exhaust,
the slower wall jet, the cold stream standing between the hot one and the intake, and above all the weight
that can be brought home.

**That last quantity is the competition in one number.** At an empty weight of 24,030 pounds and an assumed
five percent control margin, direct lift permits an allowance over empty weight of 2,637 pounds and the lift
system permits 15,875. **And the small number is badly conditioned**, amplifying a one percent thrust loss
into a ten percent loss of everything the aircraft can carry home, so a fifty degree ingestion of its own
exhaust takes nearly half of it.

**But the propulsion argument is not what the article ends on, because the evidentiary one is sharper.**
Boeing flew a delta wing it had already decided not to propose. The customer was therefore shown an
aeroplane nobody intended to build and had to reason from it to one that did not exist, while the
competitor's demonstrator flew a short takeoff, a supersonic dash and a vertical landing in a single sortie
in the configuration it was offering.

**A fly-off decides between the things placed in front of it.** When one of those things is not the thing
being bought, the fly-off has answered a question adjacent to the one asked.

**And the decision determined not only which aircraft was built but which one is known.** Among the three thousand five hundred and twenty-eight research records this article cites, the winner appears in thirty-eight titles across two decades and the
loser in one, written by its engine supplier, after the result was announced.
**An aeroplane that flew one hundred and forty-four times, went supersonic, refuelled in the air and landed vertically left almost no public technical trace**,
and that silence is the last thing the competition decided.
